OIL FUTURES: Crude dips after Trump says new strikes on Iran `highly unlikely'
Diplomatic efforts fuel de-escalation hopes
Persian Gulf exports drop below 5 million b/d in Oct
Bahri Oil sends four ships via Hormuz on Nov. 10
Crude oil futures settled lower Nov. 10 amid growing expectations of a de-escalation between the US and Iran after US President Donald Trump downplayed the chances of military strikes.
NYMEX December WTI settled 81 cents/barrel lower at $86.04/b, and ICE January Brent declined $1.08/b to $91.49/b.
"The market has continued to unwind some of the geopolitical risk premium built into prices in recent weeks as expectations grow that the conflict between the US and Iran could move towards de-escalation," Ewa Manthey and Warren Patterson said in a note by ING Nov. 10.
Trump, speaking to reporters on Nov. 9, said that more US strikes against Iran were "highly unlikely" and that there is "positive momentum" behind efforts to end the conflict.
"Crude oil prices dipped as hopes build that recent diplomatic efforts [would lead] to an end of the Middle East conflict. [US President Donald] Trump said it is highly unlikely he would order extensive strikes on Iran while there are peace negotiations going on," ANZ Research analysts Brian Martin and Daniel Hynes wrote in a note Nov. 10.
NYMEX December RBOB settled 4.21 cents/gallon lower at $2.6088/gal, and December ULSD declined 8.39 cents/gal to $4.1309/gal.

International Crude OPEC+ output rebounds in Oct, as Middle East export recovery continues: Platts survey Production up 2.82 mil b/d on month Output hits wartime high Group still producing well below quota
OPEC+ crude output rose by 2.82 million barrels/day month over month to hit a US-Iran wartime high in October, as exports from the Middle East continued to rebound, the latest Platts OPEC+ survey from S&P Global Energy found.
OPEC+ produced 38.09 mil b/d in October, still well below output of 42.89 million b/d in February, before the US and Israel attacked Iran. The decline from February is also partially due to the UAE leaving OPEC in May.
OPEC production jumped 2.6 million b/d to 23.84 million b/d, with Saudi Arabia, Iraq and Kuwait accounting for the bulk of this increase. Middle East producers in the group are recovering production after attacks on energy infrastructure and severe shipping restrictions linked to the conflict in the region caused production to collapse earlier in 2026.
Non-OPEC participants in the OPEC+ agreement increased production by 220,000 b/d to 14.25 million b/d in October. This was led by Russia, which benefited from a two-week break in Ukrainian attacks on its refinery network in the second half of the month.
OPEC+ is grappling with volatile oil prices. Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Dated Brent at $118.76/b on Oct. 30 -- down almost $10/b compared with the start of the month. Prices have since fallen further, with Dated Brent assessed at $91.76/b
(continued on page 3)

on Nov. 9, amid signs of progress in talks to end the wars in Ukraine and the Middle East.
Despite the output increase, OPEC+ members with quotas continue to produce well below target. Production was 2.26 million b/d below quota in October, the survey showed.
Amid lower production volumes, a group of seven producers implementing voluntary cuts kept quotas unchanged for October.
The voluntary cutters, which include Saudi Arabia, Russia, Iraq, Kuwait, Kazakhstan, Algeria and Oman, plan to keep quotas at the same level until the end of the year.
The voluntary cutters next plan to meet on Nov. 29. A full ministerial meeting as well as a meeting of the joint ministerial monitoring committee that oversees the agreement are scheduled for the same day, OPEC said previously.
Countries were invited to comment on significant discrepancies between official government data and Platts estimates. Some did not reply, and others directed Platts to official numbers.

Crude oil exports via Hormuz hold below 5 mil b/d for most of October
Transits through Hormuz drop to 14 ships on Nov 9 Exports fall from 5.2 to 1.4 million b/d Revolutionary Guards report tankers on fire

Crude oil exports out of the Persian Gulf and through the Strait of Hormuz held below 5 million barrels/day for most of October, ending the month at 4.4 million b/d as attacks on ships in the contested waterway slowed in recent days, according to Nov. 10 data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea.
Transits through Hormuz dropped to only 14 ships on Nov. 9, down from 32 ships at the end of October, according to CAS data. Crude exports started October at 5.2 million b/d, based on the seven-day moving average, and fell to as low as 1.4 million b/d on Oct. 8, the lowest since Sept. 14, according to CAS data.
More recent data, which is subject to change, showed exports dropped to 1.8 million b/d on Nov. 7. Before the Iran war started in late February, crude exports were averaging 18 million b/d at the end of 2025, according to CAS data.
Refined products exports averaged 486,265 b/d on Oct. 31, down from 5.37 million b/d on Dec. 31, according to CAS.
Reported ship attacks have recently slowed, with neither the United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations (UKMTO) Centre, US Central Command and the International Maritime Organization reporting any incidents since Nov. 8.
Bahri Ship Management President Khalid Alhammad, speaking at a Dubai conference Nov. 10, said Bahri had successfully sent four ships through Hormuz earlier in the day, even as Iran's Revolutionary Guards had begun naming ships ahead of possible attacks.
While CENTCOM has said there are no active mines in Hormuz, Iran's state television cited a Nov. 10 statement from the Revolutionary Guards that two oil tankers were on fire inside Hormuz and stranded after taking the route closer to Oman than to Iran.
-- Claudia Carpenter, Aresu Eqbali
Physical Brent sinks to five-month low against futures amid European crude glut
Brent/futures spread swings into contango Freight, Gulf oil constrain North Sea-Asia flows Brent basket crudes going unsold: Platts data
Physical Brent crude fell to its weakest level against futures in about five months, as surplus barrels build in Europe and high freight costs and cheaper Middle Eastern crude curb opportunities to move North Sea supply into Asia.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

November 10, 2026

The physical Brent/Futures spread fell to a $2.12/barrel discount on Nov. 9, according to data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, while an unusual number of North Sea cargoes have been left unsold.
Before early July, physical Brent had not traded at such a steep discount to futures since 2022 at the height of the coronavirus pandemic.
It also reflects a collapse since early October, when the Brent/ Futures spread was in strong backwardation at over $30/b.
A similar effect was occurring in the Brent prompt structure, with the one-week-ahead Brent CFD crashing by about $32/b since Oct. 7, putting it almost in contango. That reflects weakening prompt Dated Brent versus forward Brent, and therefore a well-supplied market.
In the week ended Nov. 9, the Dated Brent differential -- the most competitive among Brent, Forties, Oseberg, Ekofisk, Troll and WTI Midland -- sank to its lowest level since 2020.

These spreads have been highly volatile since the start of the US-Iran war in late February, which has roiled crude markets by disrupting flows through the Strait of Hormuz, boosting prices and slashing global inventories, and prompting regular spasms in the futures market on expectations of either peace talks or further escalation.
However, the recent erosion in prompt demand for Dated Brent has been driven mainly by high freight rates, which have constrained the eastward flow of North Sea crudes and WTI Midland.
At the same time, competitively priced Middle Eastern barrels -- now coming out of the Persian Gulf at greater levels, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data -- have stifled arbitrage opportunities for North Sea and US crude to clear into Asia.
With reduced Asian demand for WTI Midland crude in recent weeks, more US oil is making its way to Europe, adding to an already well-supplied Northwest European crude market, although traders note that the US can absorb excess barrels.
And an equally well-supplied Mediterranean crude market -- flush with cheap CPC Blend from Kazakhstan -- is not serving as an outlet for barrels from the Americas, but is rather pushing transatlantic volumes toward the North Sea.
Unsold cargoes These factors have led North Sea barrels to go without buyers.
Platts data shows 31 offers for crudes within the Dated Brent basket left outstanding at the London close across 10 sessions between Oct. 27 and Nov. 9.
On Nov. 9, traders continued to report unsold Novemberloading North Sea cargoes and scant activity for December-

CHINA DATA: October crude imports rebound to 7-month high
Middle East Gulf deliveries rise to 4.5 mil b/d: CAS Oil companies prioritize operations over stockpiling Clean product exports drop 27.7% on month
China's crude imports continued to recover in October, reaching a seven-month high of 9.92 million b/d (41.94 million metric tons), data from the General Administration of Customs showed Nov. 10.
Despite a 13.3% year-over-year decline and remaining below the 11.82 million b/d recorded in March, imports rose 9.4% from September on a barrels-per-day basis, according to GAC data.

The increase was in line with expectations as more cargo transited the Strait of Hormuz, supported by high freight rates, Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, reported earlier. The trend is likely to continue into November.
Data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea suggested that deliveries from the Middle East Gulf reached 4.5 million b/d in October, up from 3 million b/d in September and a multi-year low of 0.5 million b/d in June, and would rise to 4.6 million b/d in November.
In comparison, deliveries were about 4.8 million b/d in February before the war, CAS data showed.
Over the first 10 months of 2026, China's crude imports averaged 9.64 million b/d, down 15.1% year over year from 11.36 million b/d in the year-ago period, GAC data showed.
"Recent arrivals are mainly to support refinery runs rather than for restocking, as China has seen a relatively limited decline in crude inventories since the outbreak of the war compared with other countries," said Minmin Hu, a senior principal analyst with S&P Global Energy CERA. "Concentrated arrivals may cause some inventory fluctuations, but refiners have little incentive to build stocks at current prices."
"We still largely take a hand-to-mouth approach amid uncertainties following the US midterm election. Any move by the White House could bring volatility to the oil market, while logistical uncertainties could also affect the pace of deliveries," said a crude procurement manager at a state-run refinery, adding that the company had to increase crude imports only because its commercial crude stocks had been drawn down to levels that could no longer provide additional supplies.
China is unlikely to replenish its crude stocks unless oil prices and freight costs fall sharply.
Previously, market sources said the procurement of crude reserves would be notable when oil prices fall below $65/b and would step up when below $60/b.
Oil product exports retreat China's refined oil product exports slumped 27.7% in October
to 5.18 million mt from the 45-month high of 7.16 million mt in October, according to GAC data.
Market sources estimated clean oil product exports, comprising gasoline, gasoil and jet fuel, would fall to about 3.7 million mt in October, from 4.41 million mt in September.
Clean oil product outflows in November were expected to remain at similar levels to October, as several state-run refineries halted gasoil exports amid tight domestic supplies, while narrowing export margins following the fall in crude prices also limited outflows despite new export quota allocations.
GAC's oil product import and export data are believed to have included a basket of oil products, with gasoline, gasoil, jet fuel and fuel oil accounting for the majority, according to market sources. The breakdown of products for October will be released on Nov. 18.
In January-October, China's oil product exports fell 2% year over year to 46.59 million mt, GAC data showed.

Japan's Cosmo may increase Middle East crude oil procurement if straits reopen
Cosmo shifts crude sourcing based on straits
Middle East oil costs less than US supplies
Refinery run rates climb to 95% in Q2
Japan's Cosmo Energy Holdings Co., Ltd. will shift to more crude oil procurement from the Middle East if its tankers can go through the Bab al-Mandab Strait or the Strait of Hormuz, a senior executive said Nov. 10.
The company's crude oil procurement from non-Middle East regions is almost the same as that from the Middle East, Tomoki Iwai, senior executive officer, said at Cosmo's earnings conference for the second quarter of fiscal year 2026-27 (AprilMarch) in Tokyo.
"We can refine Middle Eastern crude oil easily at our refineries in terms of equipment configuration, and procurement costs, including freight, of North American crude oil are higher than those of Middle Eastern crude oil as Japan is further from North America than the Middle East," Iwai said.
Cosmo procures non-Middle Eastern crude oil from the US and Mexico, according to Iwai.
"Cosmo increased crude oil procurement from non-Middle Eastern regions in the summer as it was hard for our tankers to go through the Bab al-Mandab Strait after July," Iwai said.
"Equipment configuration at Cosmo's refineries is highly compatible with Middle Eastern crude oil, so we will consider the equipment configuration after evaluating how the Bab al-Mandab or Hormuz reopens," Iwai said on Cosmo's future equipment investment for its refineries.
"We will take the best course of action, while assessing the US, Israel or the other Middle Eastern countries," Iwai said.
Oil product policies "Cosmo may import kerosene cargoes if domestic kerosene
demand rises sharply, though we have secured the cargoes as is the case every year by increasing kerosene stocks in the summer," Iwai said.
"Cosmo has no plan to export kerosene during winter as we will focus on domestic supply in the peak season, though we plan

to export gasoil in the second half as much as in the first half," Iwai said about Cosmo's export strategy over October-December.
According to Iwai, Cosmo exported around 280,000 kiloliters of gasoil in the first half of FY 2026-27.
"Run rates at Cosmo's refineries rose to 95% over July to September in comparison to a little less than 90% over April to June, as we have accumulated knowledge on how to blend Middle Eastern crude oil with North American crude oil," Iwai said.
Asked about a change in Japan's oil product subsidy program, Iwai said, "The change will have few impacts in the short term unless retail prices skyrocket, as gasoline is indispensable to our daily life."
Japan's Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi said Oct. 29 that the government will begin raising its gasoline target price of about ¥170/liter ($1.08/liter) by ¥5/l from December through early spring.
Cosmo Oil Co., Ltd. is a group company of Cosmo Energy Holdings and Japan's third-largest refiner.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the average prices of gasoline, kerosene and gasoil at ¥94,600/kiloliter, ¥114,000/ kl and ¥100,600/kl, respectively, across the Chiba, Kanagawa, Chukyo and Hanshin regions Nov. 10.
-- Akihiro Gotoda
Saudi crude loadings reach wartime high in Oct as Yanbu flows climb
Yanbu port lifts surge 900,000 b/d in October
Loadings in Egypt's Sidi Kerir rise to 1.4 million b/d
China boosts imports in Oct, Saudi Aramco CEO says
Saudi Arabia's visible gross crude loadings during October reached a wartime high of 7.5 million barrels/day, according to the latest S&P Global Commodities at Sea data, as Yanbu loadings recovered rapidly from September's East-West pipeline disruption and the country used alternative export routes to move barrels around the Strait of Hormuz.
Visible Yanbu loadings averaged an estimated 2.8 million b/d in October, up 900,000 b/d from a wartime low of 1.9 million b/d in September, according to the data, when an attack on the East-West pipeline interrupted shipments from Saudi Arabia's Red Sea coast.
The 7.5 million b/d total includes re-exports from Egypt and ship-to-ship transfers outside the Strait of Hormuz.
Saudi Arabia's East-West oil pipeline, with a capacity of up to 7 million b/d, was attacked on Sept. 10 along its Riyadh and Madinah segments, leading to its shutdown the following day, according to a Sept. 11 statement from the Saudi Press Agency.
Despite market estimates that repairs would take weeks for the pipeline to operate again, flows to the country's western ports resumed "within days," Saudi Arabian Oil Co. CEO Amin Nasser told analysts during a third-quarter results call on Nov. 3.
The pipeline returned to operations within "five or six days" after being attacked and was pumping 5.8 million b/d of crude Oct. 6, state media reported Saudi energy minister Prince Abdulaziz bin Salman as saying that day.

Visible Saudi crude loadings from Yanbu resumed Sept. 23 after appearing to pause on Sept. 12, according to CAS.
At the east coast, detected crude loadings from the Gulffacing Ras Tanura port declined from 2.4 million b/d over September to around 2 million b/d in October, CAS data showed.
Alternative routes Of the volumes loaded from the Gulf, Saudi Arabia has opted
to conduct ship-to-ship transfers outside the Strait of Hormuz in an effort to support supplies "for customers who are not willing to come and load from [Saudi Arabian] facilities," according to Nasser on Nov. 3.
CAS detected lower visible ship-to-ship transfers of Saudi crude over October, with 224,100 b/d of Saudi crude shuttled from within the Gulf then transferred to other tankers off Sohar, Oman and near 200,000 b/d collected off Fujairah, UAE over October.
Additional visible Saudi crude STS transfers outside the Middle East Gulf in October included around 575,700 b/d off Vadinar, India, and an estimated 43,800 b/d off Port Said in Egypt, CAS data showed.
Roughly 1.9 million b/d of crude shipped from Yanbu port in October was routed to Egypt, with 1.7 million b/d arriving at Ain Sukhna terminal and around 106,300 b/d arriving at Port Said, according to CAS.
Re-exports of Saudi crude from Egypt's Sidi Kerir terminal, which typically arrive from Yanbu to Ain Sukhna terminal on the Red Sea and are transported to the Mediterranean port via the SUMED pipeline, also climbed in October, according to CAS.
Sidi Kerir loadings reached 1.4 million b/d over October, up from the previous month's volume of just 610,700 b/d, as customers were reportedly met with delivery cancellations while Yanbu flows were interrupted over September.
Port Said re-exports reached 84,300 b/d over the month, CAS data showed, joining Saudi Arabia's list of alternative gateways for shipping its crude with a second month of visible loadings in a row.
China returns Aramco stated it would continue to utilize all its available
export routes to ship its crude, whether that be "through the Red Sea or through the Mediterranean using the SUMED [pipeline], or the Suez Canal and Port Said, or through the Gulf," Nasser said during the Nov. 3 earnings call.
Nasser also said that Chinese imports of Saudi crude had "come back" in October, with expectations that demand from the Asian buyer would climb further amid the current global supply crisis.
Overall, Middle East Gulf crude deliveries to China climbed to 4.5 million b/d in October, up from 3 million b/d in September and from a multiyear low of 500,000 b/d in June, according to CAS data.
"[Oil] demand will come back stronger, not only to meet base demand growth, but also to build cushion for energy security -- a priority that is now at the top of every country's agenda. We've

seen [that] China, now, has indeed come back as a major crude oil buyer," Nasser said.
The volume of Saudi crude loaded during October and destined for China reached 1.6 million b/d, according to CAS.
CAS could revise the estimates as more data becomes available. Saudi crude transported aboard ships with their automatic identification systems switched off may not always be captured in the estimates.

Kazakh CPC Blend crude loadings set to average 1.4-1.5 million b/d in Dec: traders
Dec set to consist mostly of Suezmax-sized stems
Market remains muted amid oversupply
Kazakh-origin CPC Blend crude loadings are set to average between 1.4-1.5 million barrels/day in December, according to data collated by Mediterranean crude traders with direct knowledge of loading schedules.
The Caspian Pipeline Consortium stopped releasing full loading schedules in early January 2023 and began communicating load dates directly to individual lifters.
The December program is set to be mostly of Suezmax-sized 130,000 metric ton clips, with just two Aframax-sized parcels of 95,000 mt, according to two traders.
One of the two traders noted that the December volume was slightly shorter than November's, while a third trader said loadings should be closer to the November program.
Platts assessed CPC Blend CIF Augusta Suezmax at an $8.325/b premium to Dated Brent Nov. 9, up by 50 cents/b day over day.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Ernest Puey

BP offers January Murban crude
Four Dubai partials trade during MOC
Cash differentials for Middle East sour crude markers sank back toward three-month lows during the Singapore Platts Market on Close assessment process Nov. 10, while selling interest for January Murban crude was seen.

During the MOC process, four January Dubai partials, each of 25,000 barrels, traded. Mercuria was the sole seller, while the buyers were BP, Vitol and PetroChina.

Americas Crude
ExxonMobil signs MOU to develop oil fields in Venezuela
Joint development of onshore, deepwater, undeveloped fields
PDVSA's output expected to reach 1.3 million b/d by year-end
ExxonMobil and the Venezuelan state-owned oil company PDVSA signed a memorandum of understanding to develop oil fields in the South American country during a ceremony held in Caracas on the evening of Nov. 9, which was broadcast on the official channel Venezolana de Televisión.
"This is the first major step toward returning to Venezuela's oil and gas sector after months of constructive dialogue. It is a project that seeks to combine technology and resources to drive development and benefit society as a whole," said Dan Ammann, president of ExxonMobil Upstream Company, following the signing of the memorandum of understanding.
"We believe that our unique capabilities, combined with this country's extraordinary resources and Venezuela's long history of oil and gas production, will bring significant benefits to all parties involved. We have a lot of work ahead of us," Ammann added.
ExxonMobil was represented by its executive director for Venezuela, Andrés Acosta, and PDVSA by its president, Héctor Obregón. The ceremony was attended by Acting President Delcy Rodríguez; the US Chargé d'affaires in Venezuela, John Barrett and David Juárez-Navarro, senior director at ExxonMobil.
ExxonMobil withdrew from Venezuela in 2007 following the expropriation of its assets under the administration of the late Hugo Chávez and initiated international arbitration.
"The agreement is the result of months of bilateral negotiations and provides for the joint development of fields in onshore areas, as well as in deepwater and undeveloped areas, with estimated reserves of billions of barrels of oil," said Rodríguez.
"We know that ExxonMobil has the technical capacity, cuttingedge technology, and necessary capital to move these projects

forward," she added. In January, during a meeting with President Donald Trump
at the White House, ExxonMobil CEO Darren Woods openly described Venezuela as uninvestable under the commercial and legal conditions in place at the time.
In late January, just a few weeks after former President Nicolás Maduro was captured in Caracas by US military commandos, Venezuela amended its hydrocarbons law, thereby eliminating the state monopoly on hydrocarbon exploitation.
PDVSA estimated Venezuela will reach a production level of 1.3 million b/d as of Dec. 31, according to Hector Obregón, the company's president, who spoke to business leaders in Caracas on Oct. 9.
In October, the average production by PDVSA and its foreign partners, including Productive Production Contracts (CPPHs), rose to 1.230 million b/d, up from 1.218 million b/d in September, according to preliminary data from the Ministry of Hydrocarbons, reviewed by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
The CPPH were incorporated as a business model in the reform of the hydrocarbons law. Venezuela has entered into 61 strategic agreements in the oil and gas sector. Currently, there are 44 negotiations underway, and the company expects to close the year 2026 with a total of 105 signed contracts, according to previous reports.
-- Mery Mogollon
Occidental exceeds production targets using new drilling technology
New Mexico output surges 23% since January
AI surveillance cuts well failures by 22%
Company identifies 4 billion boe in EOR resources
Occidental Petroleum beat its own US onshore production targets in the third quarter as the company rolled out a technology-driven strategy to unlock billions of barrels of additional resources across its Permian Basin, Gulf of America, and international portfolio, the company said Nov. 10.
US onshore production outperformed the midpoint of guidance by 20,000 barrels of oil equivalent/day in the quarter, driven by record output in New Mexico and a surge in Powder River Basin volumes, Oxy CEO Richard Jackson said during a third-quarter earnings call.
In New Mexico, Occidental reported record gross operated production of 284,000 boe/d, up about 23% since January, according to the company's earnings materials. In the Powder River Basin, the company brought a record 27 wells online in the third quarter, driving more than 70% production growth versus the first quarter to average net production of 23,000 boe/d in the third quarter.
"Across US unconventional, our 2025 and 2026 wells have delivered top-tier performance in every basin with at least 10% above the industry average on a six-month cumulative oil per lateral foot basis," Jackson said.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

8

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

November 10, 2026

In the Delaware Basin, Jackson said, increasing the number of wells per bench in some areas maintained individual well performance while boosting recovery per drill spacing unit by up to 2.5 times, a development that could materially increase recoverable volumes without similar increases in capital spending.
On enhanced oil recovery, Jackson said the company holds 2 billion boe of resource, much of it on its 1.2 million-acre Permian Central Basin Platform position.
The company is applying unconventional drilling and fracking techniques to historically bypassed conventional zones and drilled eight conventional grassroots horizontal wells in 2026 that are competitive within its portfolio, featuring high oil-cut production and lower decline rates.
Sidetrack program An EOR sidetrack program launched in late February had
drilled about 80 sidetracks by the end of the third quarter, with 500 additional sidetrack candidates identified.
The sidetrack program and conventional horizontals together added 10,000 boe/d as of the end of the third quarter, according to the company's earnings report. New conventional horizontal opportunities contribute more than 200 million boe of development-ready resource, with further upside through appraisal and CO2 EOR, Jackson said.
In unconventional EOR, Occidental has identified another 2 billion boe of resources. Its first three commercial projects are progressing toward injection by 2029, with more than 30 additional development-ready opportunities in the pipeline.
These projects over the last 10 years have demonstrated about 45% production uplift, with broader technology combinations showing potential to increase estimated ultimate recovery by 70% to 140% per well.
"We have identified roughly 450 million boe of waterflood resource at less than $40 breakeven, providing more than a decade of development runway today," Jackson added, referring to the Gulf of Mexico.
Occidental's first Gulf waterflood project, the King dumpflood, began first injection in July. Third-party gas pipeline constraints and maintenance activity affected regional third-quarter production. Production was fully restored by late October.
International volumes were slightly lower in the quarter, mainly because of a change in gas processing mode to ethane rejection at Dolphin in the Middle East.
CO2 strategy "We have signed offtake agreements with two project
developers to deliver 2 million metric tons per year of anthropogenic CO2 captured from third-party gas processing plants for use in our Permian EOR operations, which represent about 15% of our 2026 purchased CO2," Jackson said.
Around 50 million tons per year of CO2 are available from proximate sources, including around 10 million tons per year from gas processing and more than 15 million tons per year

from natural gas power generation in the Permian, according to company estimates.
On digital operations, Occidental said about 9,000 wells, or 68% of its artificial lift wells, are under AI surveillance with predictive intelligence, supporting up to 7% annual production uplift and reducing failure frequency by 22% versus 2023.
About 50% of that AI-surveilled subset is operating autonomously, including roughly 450,000 barrels/day of production, particularly in the Delaware Basin. In Oman, 600,000 barrels per day of steam injection is now optimized well by well and pattern by pattern using these tools, Occidental said.
Stratos outlook On its Stratos direct air capture plant, Occidental said it was
making progress on non-technology-related repairs and had captured more than 2,000 metric tons of atmospheric CO2 to build pellet inventory.
The company said Stratos remained on track to begin full plant startup around the end of the year and transition to operations in 2027.
"Spend next year will be minimal," COO Kenneth Dillon said. For the fourth quarter, Occidental expects lower gas marketing income as additional Permian gas takeaway capacity comes online. Narrower Waha-to-Gulf Coast spreads affected midstream earnings in the quarter, though this was largely offset by Permian gas realizations in the oil and gas segment.

Brazil extends gasoline, hydrous ethanol tax relief through Dec. 9
Federal taxes remain zero on gasoline, hydrous ethanol
Hydrous producer subsidy remains at 0.428 real/liter
Decision removes near-term uncertainty from ethanol market
Brazil's federal government extended tax relief on gasoline and hydrous ethanol for another 30 days, maintaining the current fuel support framework through Dec. 9, according to a decree published late Nov. 9.
Decree 13.159, signed by acting President Geraldo Alckmin, extends the zero rates for PIS/Pasep and Cofins on gasoline imports and domestic sales, as well as the zero CideCombustíveis rate. Gasoline used in aviation remains excluded.
For hydrous ethanol, PIS/Pasep and Cofins will also remain at zero through Dec. 9. The tax exemption is equivalent to about 0.19 real/liter, while the gasoline measures represent federal tax relief of about 0.89 real/liter.
The government also extended the extraordinary economic subsidy paid to hydrous ethanol producers and producer cooperatives at 0.428 reaias/liter through Dec. 9. The subsidy is intended to preserve ethanol's competitive tax differential against fossil fuels and can be extended again by the Finance Ministry.
The measures had been scheduled to expire Nov. 9. The government said the extension was aimed at limiting inflationary pressure from potentially higher gasoline and ethanol prices amid elevated international crude oil prices linked to the conflict in the Middle East.
The decision came after uncertainty over the future of the measures sharply reduced activity in Brazil's Center-South ethanol spot market on Nov. 9.
Trading was limited during the session, with few offers and, consequently, few deals concluded. Market participants said uncertainty over the gasoline tax relief and hydrous ethanol

subsidy was the main reason buyers and sellers remained on the sidelines.
Some producers withheld offers altogether while awaiting a government decision, limiting price discovery. Buyers similarly showed little urgency to commit fresh volumes before knowing whether the existing fuel policy would remain in place.
The extension was said late Nov. 9 and subsequently formalized in an extra edition of Brazil's official gazette. The government's Casa Civil published confirmation of the measure at 10:18 pm local time.
Market participants expect the decision to remove an important source of near-term uncertainty and allow ethanol spot activity to normalize, with the current tax and subsidy structure now preserved for another month.
The extension also avoids an immediate change in the relative economics between hydrous ethanol and gasoline at the pump. The hydrous subsidy had been raised to 0.428 real/ liter in October after the government fully zeroed federal gasoline taxes, with policymakers seeking to preserve the competitive differential in favor of the biofuel.
The Center-South market enters the new subsidy period with a comparatively tight supply-demand balance. Recent agriculture ministry data showed anhydrous ethanol inventories well below year-ago levels, while hydrous stocks declined during the first half of October despite continued production as strong distributor withdrawals absorbed much of the new supply.
Spot ethanol prices have also strengthened substantially since the beginning of October, supported by firm producer offers, robust downstream demand and weather-related disruptions to cane harvesting.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed daily hydrous ethanol (E100) ex-mill Ribeirão Preto at 3,290 reais/cubic meter, up 10 reais day over day, while anhydrous ethanol was assessed at 3,800 reais/cubic meter, also up 10 reais. Ribeirão Preto is the main inland pricing reference for ethanol in São Paulo state.
Hydrous ethanol delivered CIF Paulínia was assessed at 3,300 reais/cubic meter, unchanged day over day. The assessment reflects product delivered into Paulínia -- a key fuel distribution hub in Greater São Paulo -- via truck, rail, pipeline or direct tank transfer.
-- Vinicius Damazio

disruptions expose weaknesses in infrastructure and supply arrangements, a Shell Aviation executive said Nov. 10 at the IATA Aviation Energy Forum in Guangzhou.
Conventional jet will remain central to aviation for decades, requiring investment in alternative supplies, trading capabilities and storage to strengthen energy security, Doris Tan, head of Shell Aviation Asia-Pacific and Middle East, said at a panel.
"Conventional jet is the main fuel that will be needed in aviation for many decades to come," Tan said. "We need to really invest in supply contingency and the infrastructure projects."
Middle Eastern jet/kerosene production is projected to remain below 815,000 barrels/day in the fourth quarter, compared with 1.05 million b/d a year earlier, according to S&P Global Energy CERA's November aviation outlook. Refined product exports through the Strait of Hormuz are expected to remain low through year-end and potentially into the first half of 2027.
The disruption is also feeding into Asian jet fuel economics with Singapore jet/kerosene-Dubai cracks strengthening $10.4/b to $66.2/b in October, according to CERA. Amid curtailed Middle distillate exports from the Middle East and higher freight costs weighing on incentives to produce refined products for export, CERA expects Asian jet/kerosene cracks to remain elevated in November and December.
Executives from United Airlines, IAG and storage operator Vopak also called for closer coordination and longer-term investment decisions to address supply-chain vulnerabilities.
Having a nearby refinery offers supply security but needs to be complemented by trading capabilities that allow buyers to source elsewhere if local production becomes unavailable or refiners shift output from jet fuel to diesel, Tan said.
Competition with diesel is affecting jet availability. CERA forecasts European jet production to fall to just over 1.1 million b/d in the fourth quarter from 1.3 million b/d in the third, as refiners shift their production focus toward diesel following the summer travel peak.
Alternative sourcing can entail longer voyages and different shipping routes, potentially increasing requirements for tanks and other midstream infrastructure, Tan said.
Suppliers also face uncertainty over demand, creating financial exposure even when they can secure and deliver fuel, she added. More timely exchanges of supply and demand information, alongside additional buffer time in fuel planning, could help manage those risks.
Cost of resilience United Airlines managing director of fuel management Reuben
Raina said airlines' emphasis on cost and lean supply chains could leave airports dependent on a single supply route.
Additional routes need to be genuinely independent and provide sufficient capacity to make a meaningful difference during disruptions, he said. Small-scale trucking alternatives might offer limited protection for airports handling large fuel volumes.
"I am a big proponent of diversifying," Raina said.

Airlines should weigh the potential cost of failing to invest against the immediate expense of additional infrastructure, rather than focusing primarily on quarterly or annual budgets, he said.
Opportunities to repurpose terminals, tanks or pipelines can arise in limited windows following a crisis, while securing stakeholder support, permits and funding takes time, Raina added. Spreading capital costs over longer periods could help make investments more acceptable to users.
Tan said conversations with airport authorities had become more encouraging after they experienced supply strains, with some taking a longer-term view of infrastructure needs.
"The window of opportunity is indeed small, but it is here," she said.
Matching investment and supply risks Vopak manager Stephen Hoh said newer terminals were
designed to allow tank infrastructure to be modified or upgraded for different fuels. That flexibility allowed Vopak to adapt tankage for jet fuel storage and redistribution amid disruptions, he said.
Infrastructure accessible to multiple users could also lower costs across the supply chain, Hoh added.
IAG group head of fuel operations Steffan Munday, however, cautioned the investment needed to address the specific constraint in each supply chain. Additional airport tanks alone would not resolve inadequate delivery capacity or a shortage of available fuel.
European supply strains have also highlighted distribution constraints. Low Rhine water levels curtailed imports and contributed to German jet stock draws in the second and third quarters despite higher production and weak demand, according to CERA. Reduced barge loadings and limited road and rail alternatives continued to constrain inland deliveries heading into November.
IAG's self-supply arrangements had improved its visibility into the supply chain and its ability to respond early to disruptions, Munday said. "I think we were much more aware of what was going on within the supply chain."
For import-dependent markets, a few days' warning might be insufficient to arrange replacement supplies, reinforcing the importance of earlier visibility, he added.
-- Mia Pei

CAAC said in an accompanying explainer that six companies had completed SAF production airworthiness certification, the safety and technical approval, representing 1.2 million-1.4 million metric tons/year of capacity. The 2030 target implies an increase of about 71%-100% from that stated base.

More pathways for energy security China Southern Air Holding executive vice president Chen Zhe
also highlighted in his keynote at the forum the country's used cooking oil and agricultural residue resources, renewable-energy capacity, manufacturing base and large domestic market as foundations for SAF development.
The plan by CAAC targets more than 20,000 metric tons/year of capacity using feedstocks other than waste oils by 2030.
It calls for upgrades to waste-oil processing to increase yields and lower costs, while accelerating the commercialization of cellulosic biomass pathways.
CAAC also called for the development of power-to-liquid production using carbon dioxide and clean, low-carbon hydrogen, and alcohol-to-jet technologies based on non-food biomass.
Idahosa said broader production across regions could strengthen aviation's energy security by diversifying fuel sources and supply chains.
"More SAF produced in more places means greater energy security," he said.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed SAF FOB China at $2,035/mt on Nov. 6, up $3/mt from the day prior.
Building delivery infrastructure Besides production targets, CAAC's plan highlighted the
development of SAF storage, blending and refuelling facilities. It calls for upgrades to refuelling systems, a digital operations
management platform, and support for Chinese-made civil aircraft to conduct high-blend SAF validation.
The regulator plans to formulate a SAF action plan, gradually expand application pilots and develop blending targets, as part of priority projects aimed at optimizing civil aviation's energy mix. CAAC also plans to establish sustainability-certification standards and strengthen international mutual recognition.
Chen called for cooperation on fuel standards, sustainability certification and accounting, citing high production costs and

remains central to implementation, Idahosa said. "Capacity on paper does not decarbonize air transport. Molecules delivered to airports do."

DHL Express launches SAF flights from Paris in TotalEnergies deal
DHL Express uses SAF at Paris CDG hub
Deal cuts 11,800 metric tons CO2 annually TotalEnergies supplies 5 million liters under pact
DHL Express has become the first express cargo carrier to use sustainable aviation fuel at Paris Charles de Gaulle Airport, launching SAF-powered flights to key European and African hubs under a new supply agreement with TotalEnergies that could reshape fuel procurement patterns for the air freight sector.
The one-year renewable deal, announced Nov. 9, covers 5 million liters of SAF and marks DHL Express' first use of the low-carbon fuel in France. The agreement is expected to reduce carbon dioxide equivalent emissions by approximately 11,800 metric tons annually, with SAF-powered flights operating from the Paris CDG hub to Leipzig, Casablanca, Milan and London -- routes that sit at the core of DHL's European express network.
The deal signals growing commercial momentum for SAF offtake agreements in the air cargo segment, where demand for verifiable emissions reductions is accelerating ahead of tightening regulatory requirements in the European Union. By anchoring SAF supply at one of Europe's busiest cargo airports, the agreement could encourage rival express operators to pursue similar arrangements at major hub airports, intensifying competition for available SAF volumes.
"This latest milestone is part of DHL's global strategy to decarbonize express transportation and help our customers reduce their transport-related indirect emissions," Travis Cobb, EVP Global Network Operations & Aviation at DHL Express, said. "In 2025, SAF already accounted for 10% of the aviation fuel used across DHL's own aircraft fleet, positioning our Group as a leader in both logistics and aviation sustainability."
Supply context The SAF supplied under the agreement is produced from
waste and residual materials, including used cooking oil, and can reduce greenhouse gas emissions by around 80% over its lifecycle compared with conventional fossil jet fuel, according to the press release. The fuel requires no modifications to existing aircraft or airport infrastructure, lowering barriers to adoption across the sector.
TotalEnergies, which supplies the SAF, framed the deal as part of a broader industrial transformation strategy. "Sustainable

ICE LSGO hits lowest since August on Iran de-escalation hopes
LSGO drops to August low on Iran hopes
Trump signals de-escalation momentum
Geopolitical risk premium unwinds
Front-month ICE LSGO futures fell to their lowest level since August on Nov. 10, pressured by a broader decline across the oil complex as the market continued to pare back the geopolitical risk premium tied to the US-Iran conflict.
The ICE LSGO front-month contract settled at $1,282.5/mt, down $47.75/mt on the day. This is the lowest assessed value since Aug. 28, when it was assessed at $1,280/mt.
The decline followed comments from US President Donald Trump, who said there was "positive momentum" toward ending the conflict after meeting Qatari Prime Minister Sheikh Mohammed bin Abdulrahman Al Thani. "We are seeing positive momentum toward an end to this Conflict and will ensure that America's interests are at all times protected," the US president said in a post on his social media platform Truth Social. He also indicated that further US military strikes on Iran were unlikely for the time being, although Washington would maintain its naval blockade.
"The market has continued to unwind some of the geopolitical risk premium built into prices in recent weeks as expectations grow that the conflict between the US and Iran could move towards de-escalation," Ewa Manthey and Warren Patterson said in an ING note on Nov. 10.
Observed and assumed vessel movements through the Strait of Hormuz eased to 14 transits on Nov. 9, from 16 the previous day. At Bab al-Mandab, traffic rose to 38 crossings from 36 a day earlier, according to the latest data from S&P Global MINT and S&P Global Commodities at Sea. Disruptions to trade routes and attacks on transiting vessels have added a significant risk premium, which has continued to contribute to elevated freight rates. Platts assessed Clean Arab Gulf-UKC 90kt LR2 freight at $211.11/mt on Nov.10. This remains significantly above the level reported in August, which did not exceed $110/mt, peaking at $108.06/mt on Aug.31.

Market participants are also monitoring talks brokered by the US between Russia and Ukraine in Abu Dhabi, their first direct negotiations since February. Any progress toward a settlement could further reduce pressure on European supply.
"Away from the Middle East, disruption to energy infrastructure remains a source of uncertainty. Ukrainian attacks on Russian refining capacity have continued alongside Russian strikes on Ukraine's energy system," according to the same note from ING.
Risk premiums continue to support prices across the middle distillate complex, despite the recent downward movement. Open interest, as reported by ICE on Nov. 9, declined across the front months but increased in Q1 2027, indicating that trading interest shifted further along the curve amid heightened volatility and uncertainty over the geopolitical outlook.
-- Oskar Leskow, David Neef
ICE LSGO managed-money shorts rise 8.7% as net long position falls
Managed money cuts net long positions 12%
Short positions jump 8.7% in gasoil market
Producers reduce net short exposure significantly
Managed-money participants cut their net long exposure to ICE LSGO futures and options by 12% in the week ended Nov. 3, as traders continued to reduce bullish exposure to the European gasoil market per the most recent ICE Commitments of traders report published Nov. 6.
Long positions classified under managed money fell by 7.5% week over week to 81,909 contracts, from 88,523 contracts on Oct. 27. Short positions meanwhile increased by 1,700 contracts, or 8.7%, to 21,164 contracts. As a result, the group's net long position fell to 60,745 contracts, from 69,059 contracts a week earlier. The simultaneous reduction in longs and increase in shorts points to a more bearish shift in positioning than what was seen in the week prior, where the decline in net length was driven primarily by a reduction in long exposure.
Managed money spread trading positions increased by 2% week over week to 85,137 contracts, suggesting spread strategies remained prominent even as participants further reduced outright bullish exposure.
Across other trader categories, swap dealers' net position was little changed, increasing by 258 contracts to a net long of 231,723 contracts. 'Other reportables' became 6,615 contracts more net short, with their position widening to 231,672 contracts, from 225,057 contracts the previous week.
Producers/merchants became notably less net short in ICE gasoil futures and options over the week. Long positions increased by 9.3% to 209,430 contracts, while shorts edged up 0.7% to 278,019 contracts. Their net short position consequently narrowed to 68,589 contracts, from 84,456 contracts on Oct. 27, highlighting how producers remain exposed to upside risk as the market remains volatile.

Platts European Fuel Oil Daily Market Analysis
VLSFO supply tightens across Europe
Dirty tanker freight rates fall steadily
HSFO availability views differ by region
European very low sulfur fuel oil markets remained tight across Northwest Europe and the Mediterranean Nov. 10, amid steady arbitrage flows out of Europe.
Falling dirty tanker freight rates have supported vessel fixtures out of Europe to Singapore and the Americas.
In the Mediterranean, a lack of supply has led to elevated premiums for VLSFO-delivered cargoes.
Market participants said one entity seeking a delivered VLSFO cargo for the East Mediterranean region could not find an offer.
"The market is short [for] last-decade November," one participant said.
Meanwhile, demand for low sulfur fuel oil products had been supported by VLSFO prices, contributing to a rising hi-lo paper spread.
Perspectives surrounding European high sulfur fuel oil supply were mixed on the day.
Freight economics had been playing a role, noting that "high freight rates ... [are] making the arbitrage difficult," though the same trader maintained "there is no tightness."
Mediterranean participants, by contrast, pointed to limited availability.
"I would say the HS market is tight," a Mediterranean-based trader said. "Next month the resupply will be more balanced" on the back of arrivals from Canada and Venezuela.
In the retail bunker market, meanwhile, demand remained lackluster as peak season drew to a close.
Trading activity in the Platts Market on Close assessment process was quiet Nov. 10, with 6,000 metric tons traded in the Rotterdam VLSFO barge MOC and 4,000 mt for the HSFO equivalent.
There were no trades for fuel oil cargoes. Platts is part of S&P Global Energy.

Tankers
US Treasury may allow purchase of sanctioned tankers on case basis
OFAC may be targeting 50-80 tanker deals
Higher freight rates spur a shift to compliance
The US Treasury Department may allow companies to buy sanctioned shipping vessels and return them to legitimate shipping on a case-by-case basis, according to a government source and a person with knowledge of the matter.
"Treasury may consider proposals from legitimate shipping actors to purchase and restore shadow fleet vessels to the licit shipping industry on a case-by-case basis," a US official told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
This approach "ensures that no sanctioned person receives any benefit, and reduces the size of the fleet overall, while helping address pressures on the global energy market," the US official said.
Another source with knowledge of the matter said Treasury's Office of Foreign Assets Control could be targeting 50-80 sanctioned tankers owned by non-sanctioned companies. There could be 20-30 owners willing to move quickly, the source said.
At the end of September, there were a total of 1,225 tankers

sanctioned by the US, UK or EU, S&P Global Commodities at Sea said in an Oct. 29 shadow tanker report. Of those, 560 were sanctioned by the US.
Shadow tankers are increasingly being drawn into the compliant market due to rising freight rates, according to a separate CAS market impact report issued Oct. 23.
In 2026, 41 shadow-fleet crude tankers entered compliant trade through Oct. 20, with 68% remaining fully compliant so far, compared with just 17.5% of vessels that remained compliant in 2025.
The shift to continued compliance was particularly notable in the third quarter when mainstream tanker earnings surged, the report said. Dirty Arab Gulf-China VLCC freight averaged approximately $197/mt in September, up 77% from August, the report said.
CAS defines the shadow fleet as tankers that have transported Iranian or Russian oil in the last two years, vessels subject to US sanctions, and vessels with unknown or suspected false flag registrations.
-- Kate Winston

News
EIA raises 2027 Brent oil price outlook by $2/b on Middle East supply constraints
Brent crude forecast rises to $86/barrel
Middle East shut-ins average 2.5 mil b/d
Refinery crack spreads widen through 2027
The US Energy Information Administration raised its 2027 price outlook for Brent crude oil by $2/barrel compared with last month's outlook to $86/b, amid ongoing supply constraints.
The EIA raised its Brent price outlook for next year because the agency expects global oil inventories to rebuild slightly more slowly than projected in October, EIA spokesperson Lee Tucker told Platts, a part of S&P Global Energy.
"The main reason is that we no longer expect Iran's oil production to increase in 2027," Tucker said.
The EIA also raised its gasoline outlook by 17 cents to $3.73/ gallon, and it increased its outlook for 2027 diesel prices by 54 cents to $5.03/gal, according to the agency's Short-Term Energy Outlook released Nov. 10.
Regarding gasoline and diesel prices, the EIA said it now assumes the global petroleum product market will face continued supply constraints through the end of 2027.
"These market conditions will support wider petroleum product crack spreads next year and incentivize elevated US refinery operations," the agency said.
Crude oil shut-ins in the Middle East averaged about 2.5 million b/d in October, and are expected to remain flat through the remainder of 2026, the EIA said.
"Amid ongoing constraints, we forecast Brent will average about $100/b during November and December," the agency said.
Ongoing shut-ins Export workarounds from the Middle East like pipeline and
overland bypass routes, ship-to-ship transfers and new pipeline capacity in the UAE will lead to shut-in volumes falling in 2027, the EIA said.
Middle East shut-ins are expected to average 1.9 million b/d in the first half of 2027, and 1.3 million b/d in H2 2027, the outlook said.
The majority of production in the Middle East outside of Iran is expected to return to pre-conflict averages by the end of the second quarter of 2027, but exports from Iran will remain constrained throughout the forecast, the EIA said.
Brent prices are expected to drop below $90/b in Q2 2027 and fall to an average of $77/b in Q4 2027, the outlook said.
"However, we expect crude oil flows from the Middle East -- via both the Strait of Hormuz and alternative routes -- will remain susceptible to disruption, resulting in more volatility in shortterm price movements than our monthly forecast indicates," the outlook said.
Global oil inventories fell by an average of 1.2 million b/d in Q3

2026, but will begin building in Q4 2026, the EIA said. The agency said, however, "The heightened risk of supply
disruptions and longer shipping routes will limit the near-term impact of global stock builds until geopolitical risk dissipates and inventories begin to return closer to pre-conflict levels."
Crack spreads Refinery crack spreads have been high this year due to
damage to refineries in the Middle East and Russia, and spreads will remain high for most of next year due to ongoing disruptions to global markets, the EIA said.
"We expect these disruptions to be mostly concentrated in the first quarter, with constraints easing through the remainder of the year," the agency said.
Utilization of operable capacity in 2027 is expected to be about the same as in 2026, the outlook said.
"Potential unplanned outages because of weather or other events, as well as the need to undertake maintenance that was deferred in 2026, present a source of uncertainty and downside risk to our refining forecast," the EIA said.
-- Kate Winston

Crude futures fell below $90/barrel Nov. 10 for the first time in more than two months as markets weighed growing prospects for an easing of the conflicts in Iran and Ukraine.
US President Donald Trump said Nov. 9 there was "positive momentum" toward ending the Middle East conflict, following midterm elections in which the Republican Party lost control of Congress amid public dissatisfaction with the war in Iran. The conflict has disrupted oil production and shipping through widespread attacks on energy infrastructure and restrictions on key maritime routes, driving sharp price volatility throughout 2026.
Separately, talks aimed at ending the war in Ukraine are taking

The following are key facts about the latest oil market developments.
Prices Tight inventories and supply risk have kept prices elevated,
with limited buffer if flows are disrupted again.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Dated Brent at $91.76/b on Nov. 9, down 13% since Nov. 3, when the US midterm elections were held.

It estimates that global oil markets were already in a surplus of 1.1 million b/d in September and 1.4 million b/d in October, after accounting for rising supply elsewhere and weaker demand.

month, Pertamina Deputy President Director Oki Muraza said in a discussion Nov. 9.
"Given the current global situation, with disruptions to onefifth of the world's oil supplies through the Strait of Hormuz, we are stepping up efforts to bring barrels home. For example, we have just secured an acquisition in Algeria, which is expected to add around 32,000 b/d of oil production. In theory, this would translate into one cargo arriving from Algeria in Indonesia each month," Muraza said.
The acquisition is part of Pertamina's efforts to secure oil supplies from regions outside conflict-affected areas, particularly the Middle East, he said.
Pertamina, through its affiliate PT Pertamina Algeria Eksplorasi Produksi (PAEP), agreed to acquire Spanish energy company Moeve's interests in three onshore oil fields in Algeria's Berkine Basin. The deal covers Moeve's stakes in the Rhourde el Khrouf, Bir el Msana and Ourhoud fields.
Completion remains subject to customary conditions, including approval from Algerian authorities. Neither company disclosed the transaction value, the companies said in separate statements Nov. 4.
Pertamina said Nov. 4 that the acquisition was part of its "Bring the Barrel Home" initiative, which aims to optimize production from overseas upstream assets to support oil and gas supplies to Indonesia.
In December 2025, Pertamina shipped its first 1-million-barrel crude cargo from Algeria after extending the production-sharing contract for Block 405A, securing its operations in the country for another 25 years. The cargo departed from Port Arzew in Oran.
In June 2026, a tanker carrying about 450,000 barrels of Algerian crude was scheduled to arrive at Pertamina's Cilacap refinery in Central Java to support refinery operations and domestic fuel supplies.
Outside Algeria, Pertamina also holds upstream interests in Iraq and Malaysia, with part of its international portfolio managed through its 71.09% stake in France-listed Maurel & Prom.
Expansion The company is looking to expand the overseas portfolio
further through acquisitions of oil and gas assets in North Africa and South America, as it seeks to diversify its crude supplies away from regions exposed to geopolitical conflicts, Muraza said.
"We are targeting mergers and acquisitions for oil and gas fields in North Africa, where there is no war, as well as in Latin America or South America," he said.
The company was also finalizing an expansion into Latin America through its subsidiary Maurel & Prom, Muraza said.
"We are also finalizing our move into Latin America through our subsidiary, M&P. As we continue to increase national production capacity in line with the government's target of reaching 1 million b/d by 2030, we need to secure supplies ahead of 2030," Muraza said, adding that Pertamina currently accounts for 69% of Indonesia's national oil production.
"We are pursuing significant inorganic expansion through

mergers and acquisitions, acquiring and buying oil and gas fields. We have successfully done this in North Africa, and we are now working on opportunities in South America," he said.
Alongside its overseas expansion, Pertamina is continuing to raise output from domestic fields and step up exploration, Muraza said.
"We are continuing to increase domestic production while also securing supplies before 2030. We are pursuing a fairly significant expansion through inorganic growth, including mergers and acquisitions," he said.
Domestically, Pertamina is pursuing enhanced oil recovery at the Minas field and steam flooding at the Duri field in Sumatra, while accelerating development of new discoveries and exploration activities. The company also plans to drill more than 130 exploration wells through 2030, Muraza said.
Pertamina has allocated about 75% of its capital expenditure to upstream activities, he said.
"We are working toward the president's directive to reach 1 million b/d of oil production by 2030. We are pursuing various innovations and allocating the largest share of our capital expenditure to the upstream sector, with around 75% of our capex dedicated to upstream activities," he said.
Deputy Energy and Mineral Resources Minister Yuliot Tanjung, during a meeting with the Regional Representatives Council (DPD) Nov. 9, broadcast on YouTube, said Indonesia was targeting oil production of 900,000-1 million b/d by 2029.
The target would be supported by the offering of 118 new oil and gas working areas and efforts to optimize output from existing fields using enhanced oil recovery and unconventional technologies.
-- Anita Nugraha
COP31: Big tech urged to clean up AI's energy footprint
Data centers must run on renewables, says UN
Electricity use from AI centers jumps 50% in 2025
Antalya AI pledge calls for zero-emission-powered infrastructure
Big technology companies and artificial intelligence firms face growing pressure to power their data centers with clean energy and submit to tougher disclosure rules, senior climate officials said at the UN Climate Change Conference Nov. 10, as the COP31 presidency seeks to get countries and businesses to sign a voluntary pledge to bring AI's surging energy demands in line with global climate goals.
Australia's climate change and energy minister Chris Bowen, who leads negotiations at COP31, said at a press conference on Nov. 10 that AI is "here to stay" and data centers will continue to be built, but that governments have both the right and the responsibility to set the terms.
"What rules will you put up with," Bowen said, adding that AI investment must be in countries' "national interest" and that

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

18

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

November 10, 2026

rising energy prices are "front and center" for households and businesses alike. Clean and renewable energy, he said, is the "cheapest form of energy" available, and Australia is consulting on how to ensure data centers are powered accordingly.
UN Climate Change Executive Secretary Simon Stiell, speaking at the same press conference, said the UN's position is unambiguous. "All new AI additions should be powered by renewables," Stiell said. "That is the smartest and most costeffective way of moving forward."
Antalya AI pledge The remarks came alongside the Antalya Artificial Intelligence
Pledge, released by the COP31 presidency of Turkey in late October and set to be a focus of discussions at the summit.
The voluntary pledge is open to governments, businesses, investors and civil society.
The pledge commits signatories to powering AI and data center infrastructure with clean energy, building more sustainable data centers, integrating new digital infrastructure into power grids and disclosing the environmental impacts of AI systems.
The pledge notes that electricity consumption from AIfocused data centers rose by 50% in 2025 alone, placing significant new strain on power grids and, in many markets, prolonging the life of fossil fuel generation. Data centers consume substantial amounts of electricity, with cooling requirements adding to their overall energy footprint.
The World Resources Institute welcomed the pledge's direction but called for stronger accountability. "As written, the pledge sets the right direction of travel but lacks the accountability mechanisms to steer countries and tech companies on to a better path," said Evan Tachovsky, chief data and AI officer at WRI, in a statement issued on Oct. 27.
"Responsibly managing AI requires establishing strong guardrails: powering data centers with clean energy, mandating full disclosure and requiring companies to pay for their own grid upgrades."
The pledge's first stocktake is scheduled for COP32, with WRI calling on leading countries and companies to use the intervening period to move from soft intentions to real accountability.
-- Eklavya Gupte

Platts proposes to launch new European chemically recycled naphtha assessments
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, is proposing to launch weekly assessments for refinery grade pyrolysis oil, cracker grade pyrolysis oil, and circular naphtha in Northwest Europe, effective Jan. 12, 2027.
Platts has observed continued market interest in chemically recycled naphtha market trends and pricing developments across 2026, alongside projections of increased market scale and capacity in a market that requires stronger transparency through consistent pricing benchmarks which are representative of traded specifications.
Stakeholders consider chemical recycling a key value chain for facilitating increased use of sustainable polymer and chemical materials in European manufacturing. The process is expected to be critical in allowing consumers to meet upcoming recycled content legislation in the plastics sector, such as targets from the European Union's Plastic and Packaging Waste Regulation, which will mandate minimum percentages of recycled content in plastic packaging.
The assessments would reflect the specifications and delivery terms below:

Platts amends freight calculation for Oman, Murban CFR North Asia crude assessments Nov. 2
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, has amended the freight calculation used in its daily Oman and Murban CFR North Asia crude assessments, effective Nov. 2, 2026.
Under the change, Platts is using the daily VLCC Gulf of Omanto-Far East freight assessment in $/mt (DOOEW00), launched Aug. 3, to calculate the outright prices of Oman and Murban delivered into North Asia at 4:30 pm Singapore time (0830 GMT).
The change better reflects shipping economics for the two grades, with Murban primarily loading from Fujairah and Oman from Mina Al Fahal outside the Strait of Hormuz.
Previously, the freight calculation was based on the daily VLCC Persian Gulf-to-Far East freight assessment in $/mt (TDAAB00).
Platts first proposed the change in a subscriber note published Sept. 28, 2026, followed by a decision on Oct. 15, 2026.
The freight calculation continues to use the metric ton-to-

barrel conversion factors applied to the grades, in line with their prevailing qualities. The conversion factors are currently 7.32 for Oman and 7.62 for Murban.
In line with this change, Platts is also using the daily VLCC Gulf of Oman-to-Far East Worldscale freight assessment (DIOEW00) in the freight calculation for Murban in its Indonesian Minas, Cinta and Widuri crude market parity prices (MPP).
Under the current MPP methodology for Minas, Cinta and Widuri, Murban -- as one of five basket grades -- is normalized for delivery to Singapore, accounting for freight. This was previously calculated using the daily VLCC Persian Gulf-to-Singapore worldscale freight assessment (PFAOD00).

Platts to amend rollover date of Asian Dated Brent Strip at London close
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, will amend the rollover date of its Asian Dated Brent (ADB) Strip at London close assessment to the first publication day of each month, effective Dec. 1, 2026.
The change follows a review of historical data sets and will align the ADB Strip at London close assessment with the current rollover date of the ADB Strip at Asia close assessment.
Currently, this assessment, which reflects the forward assessment of Dated Brent two months ahead of the date of publication at the 4:30 pm London close, rolls over on the 9th of the month.
Platts proposed this change in a subscriber note published Sept. 21, 2026.
The assessments affected by this change appear in Crude Oil Marketwire, Asia Pacific/Arab Gulf Marketscan, Oilgram Price Report, Platts Global Alert/ Platts Crude Oil Alert pages 2206, 2213, 2215, 2217, 2219, 2260, 2261, 2262, 2263, 2264, 2265, 2266, 2267, 2268, 2269, 2270, 2271, 2280, 2281, 2558, 2559, and under the following price database codes:

Platts amends Abu Dhabi crude OSPs publication Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, has updated its publication of
Abu Dhabi crude official selling prices effective Oct. 8, 2026, in line with changes announced by Abu Dhabi National Oil Co. (ADNOC).
In July, ADNOC said it would adopt a prompt-month pricing methodology based on the Platts Dubai benchmark (PCAAT00) plus an ADNOC-announced differential for the OSPs of its Abu Dhabi onshore and offshore crude grades, with effect from November 2026 loadings. ADNOC added that it would announce the OSP differentials for Murban, Das, Umm Lulu and Upper Zakum in the month prior to the delivery month.
Accordingly, Platts will publish the monthly OSP differentials for the four grades, basis Platts Dubai, beginning with the November differentials issued by ADNOC on Oct. 8, 2026.
Platts first announced the change in a subscriber note published on Aug. 31, 2026.
The OSP differentials are published under the following reinstated codes:

Grade Murban Umm Lulu Das Blend Upper Zakum

3-DAY TRAINING

Oil Markets Fundamentals & Commercial Strategies

Nov 3-5, 2026 | Singapore

Dec 8-10, 2026 | Dubai

Mar 23-25, 2027 | Singapore Apr 6-8, 2027 | Houston

Apr 27-29, 2026 | London

May 11-12, 2027 | Abuja

Jun 22-24, 2027 | Istanbul

Jun 30-Jul 1 | Kuala Lumpur

Oil markets are increasingly complex, shaped by shifting supply and demand, geopolitical developments, changing trade flows and price volatility. This course provides a practical understanding of the forces shaping global crude oil and refined product markets.
Explore market fundamentals, price formation and forecasting, trading, and risk management, and learn how to translate market developments into better commercial decisions.
Build the market knowledge and commercial insight to make more confident decisions in a rapidly evolving oil market.
WHO SHOULD ATTEND The course would be suitable for:
· Technical and non-technical professionals working across crude oil and refined products
· Traders, brokers and market analysts · Professionals in commercial, planning, business development,
supply and distribution roles · Shipping and logistics professionals · New industry entrants seeking a practical understanding of oil
markets

2-DAY TRAINING
Upstream Fundamentals and Economics
June 2-3 | Houston

Fossil fuels dominate the energy mix representing over 80% of global energy demand. With geopolitical tensions influencing supply volumes and lower price, the S&P Global Commodity Insights "Upstream Fundamentals and Economics" course will equip you with the insights and strategies needed to navigate a constantly changing environment, meet challenges with informed decisions, and drive business growth.
Our prestigious Upstream Fundamentals and Economics Training is a great opportunity to hear from a range of S&P Global experts sharing their expertise, regional trends and industry insights, as well as concrete case studies and analyses.
